Debates and votes of the Ditame drawn up by Commission 1st, Institutional, General Administration, Justice and Home Affairs, of the Galician Public Employment Bill
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u6	They are maintained for the full amendment of the Joint Group, the Bloc, the Parliamentary Group of the Glaga Alternative of the Left and the Parliamentary Group of the Socialists of Galicia. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u7	Thank you very much, Madam President. Good morning, gentlemen. The Galician Public Employment Act was entered into the Parliamentary Register on 19 December 2014. The Bureau of Parliament issued its publication in the Official Bulletin of the Parliament of Galicia and the opening of the deadline for the submission of amendments. Even so, listening to the Speaker's Board, he agreed to attend by the First Institutional, General Administration, Justice and Home Affairs Commission. The amendments to the whole were qualified on February 20, 2015 and the partial amendments were qualified on March 23, 2015 of the same year. The parliamentary groups of Alternative Glaga de Left, the Socialists of Galicia and the Nationalist Block Galego presented each amendment to the whole, which were rejected at the plenary session on 10 March 2015. With regard to the partial amendments, the People's Parliamentary Group made 37; the Parliamentary Group of the Socialists of Galicia, 181; the Parliamentary Group of Alternative Galicia of Left, 201; the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block of Wales, 209; and the Joint Parliamentary Group, 214. At its meeting on 26 March 2015, the Commission appointed the following rapporteurs: from the Popular Parliamentary Group, Ms Paula Prado del Río as the holder and Mr Gonzalo Trenor López as alternate; from the Parliamentary Group of Socialists of Galicia, Mr José Antonio Sánchez Bugallo as the holder and Mr Ricardo Vicente Docasar Docasar as alternate; from the Parliamentary Group of Alternative Galga de Left, Ms Yolanda Díaz Pérez as the holder and Mr José Javier Ron Fernández as alternate; from the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block Galego, Ms María Teresea Paz Franco as the holder and Ms Ana Pontón Mondelo as alternate; and from the Mixed Parliamentary Group, Ms María Consuelo Martínez García as the holder and Ms Carmen Igles Sueiro as alternate. The sitting was held on 26 March and ended its report at the meeting on 13 April 2015, a report which was discussed and adopted in Commission 1st at the meeting on 17 April 2015. The articulated text of the ditame is written in 212 articles, structured in ten titles, completed with eleven additional provisions, fifteen transitionals, two derogations and five finals. So far the work of the 1st Commission, which I am presiding over, must be completed today by submitting its ruling to plenary consideration. Nothing else and thank you very much.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u8	Thank you very much. The turn of parliamentary groups to set their position on the dictatorship and if that's the case, defend the amendments maintained. The Joint Group has the floor to Mrs. Martínez García.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u9	Thank you, President. Well, the course of this bill reflects the deeply anti-democratic character of this government and its contempt for social organizations and local institutions and disinterests it for reaching consensus. That's what explains the refusal to forward the draft law to the ESC. The democratic quality of the processing of this law is also manifested in the exclusion in practically all the articulation of the participation of trade unions after they have not opened a negotiation period with them during the processing. And, as otherwise, the capacity for dialogue and negotiation of the People's Party is demonstrated in its inability to accept the amendments tabled by both trade unions and opposition groups. The project is named after the Public Employment Act of Galicia and, however, leaves out of its scope the research and health staff in many employment-related matters, such as career, supplementary wages or mobility, rejecting all the amendments referred to in the rules of reference for these occupational collectives. Even so, it highlights the constant lack of reference to the one established in the collective implementing agreements and to the V Single Staff Convention of the Galician Union of Labour. It is curious, at least, that the text of the bill escapes its commitment to the V Single Convention, such as, for example, the rejection of Amendment No. 12 of the Joint Group, where it refuses to modify the text of the bill which says: "collective agreements of general scope or affecting various councils", which is actually established, which is the V Single Staff Convention of the Board of Galicia. At this point, the aversion of the German Government by trade union organisations and the collective bargaining that rejected all amendments that added collective agreements as labour law enforcement, included the V Single Convention of Workers of the Union or guaranteed the bargaining capacity of trade unions. But it's more: the bill aims to ensure the equality of all employees, but it refuses that this equality is collected in the V Single Convention. In addition, the bill seeks to protect the administration by seeking to establish working conditions unilaterally in case of failure to reach agreement with the union representation. Thus, in Article 13(c), he recalls: "It corresponds to the Board of Trustees to determine the instructions to which the Administration must be given in the negotiation of working conditions, setting the same for cases where there is no agreement in the negotiation." We wonder where the bargaining capacity of trade union organizations is, if there is a prior legal imposition that guarantees the Administration that, if there is no agreement, it can unilaterally impose the working conditions it wants. But even more insists on the bill to reduce the negotiating and mobilising capacity of the unions and the staff of the Greek administration by ensuring the imposition of minimum services during strikes, without collecting prior negotiation with the union representation. The contradiction between this bill and the People's Party's triumphal speech on economic recovery and the prosperity that will be this year 2015 and the following. If we are actually at the beginning of recovery, why is this bill going to be aimed at ensuring budgetary stability and cuts in public services, for example facilitating the depreciation of jobs? As for the interim staff, the treatment given to them in the bill has a lot of resemblance to an ETT scholarship. In fact, it is not considered that the need for interim personnel to exceed the limit contained in the bill is considered a structural necessity. The retention of expenditure continues, so far from considering such staff as necessary for the provision of the service, the bill ensures that, regardless of service needs, the recruitment limit of the temporary staff will never be exceeded. It also continues to condition the provision of human resources to budgetary stability, including health care. Thus, the People's Party rejected Amendment 68 of the Joint Group, which called for the annual call of all vacant jobs. That is to say, it enshrines the precariousness of work, the eventuality, the fraud in the current procurement, which is intended to be established annually and unilaterally in Article 13, without any type of criterion or prior negotiation with the union representation, the number, characteristics and remuneration of any staff. This bill also loses a great opportunity to fight against fraud and corruption in public procurement, referring only to the legal responsibility of converting into indefinite contracts, but avoiding any other delicious acts carried out from political power. And he mentions the indefinite conversion of possible contracts, because he intends to legalize the amortization of indefinite jobs earned by firm judicial judgement and his hiring with private companies. That's the only way it's understood to condition the provision of jobs to the so-called court rulings. As for the executive staff, the bill advances in the free designation without control of these positions, by not collecting the nature of the positions that these staff will occupy or which they have assigned executive functions, nor by setting a time limit for these positions, nor their working and salary conditions will be subject to collective bargaining. She insists on the bet by the places she trusts to her finger. Thus, Article 34.3 eliminates the requirement to be employed publicly to have the status of executive staff in some public entities, such as agencies, autonomics, consortia and corporate public entities; i.e. more plug-in since the party. The OPE becomes the game of the park, it can be eaten squares – i.e., amortize itself – to count twenty elsewhere and in other conditions and appoint executive staff, where no negotiation with trade union power stations is considered either. Article 49 introduces itself as a requirement to participate in selective processes in the event that workers have not been dismissed through disciplinary records of any public administration or constitutional bodies that are statutory bodies of the autonomous communities. It's a restriction on the right to public employment that has no parallel in basic legislation. This provision could therefore be unconstitutional, for violating the right to work of Article 35 of the Constitution and the constitutional order of the distribution of powers between the State and the Autonomous Community. In relation to the selection systems, the bill favors more obscurantism and greater capacity to prevent, by excluding the union representation of specialized and permanent bodies that are created for the organization of selective processes. Thus, the People's Party rejected the amendments submitted to Article 50.2, which has such an absurd text as the following: "Calls for selective processes may establish with an abstract and general nature specific requirements for access to public employment that maintain objective and proportionate relation to the functions and tasks to be performed". Even so, the bill, to protect political power further, ensures that the appointment of members of selective processes is not done by drawing. It includes in article 55.3 for selective processes interviews and medical recognitions without any kind of justification about their need, which in my view achieves the opposite effect that is said to be pursued: lack of transparency, lack of objectivity and rationality and violation of intimacy. And in case this was little, it obliges technical discretion, thus trying to limit the actions of the courts of justice. If justice gives way to technical discretion, equality of opportunity and transparency in access to public employment will disappear. It cannot be tolerated that by law it is established as a regulated code of conduct characteristics which, moreover, are called in the law principles, such as exemplaryness, austerity or honesty, hardly quantifiable or measurable. The bill loses a great opportunity to regulate access to the administration for both officials and employees, facilitating prior access to vacancies for internal promotion before its public summons, as it does not facilitate the transfer of workers and public workers near their homes, not even in the case of staff who have suffered forced mobility. As for performance assessment systems, the criteria of transparency, objectivity, impartiality and non-discrimination are not met when assessing issues as subjective as the degree of interest, initiative and effort in which public employees perform their work. How do you measure the degree of interest? Does seconding a strike mean lowering the level of interest or implying lowering the effort? Nor in this case, for the sake of such a high degree of transparency, will the participation of staff representation for the approval of performance assessment systems. It also rejected the People's Party's amendments aimed at improving the quality of life of workers and public workers, including rights that do not affect budgets, such as Amendments Nos 70, 71 and 72 of the Joint Group, aimed at improving the proximity of public staff to their homes through the ordinary contest. Let's not say already that if they were affecting budgets, like coverage of 100 percent of the salary in IT cases. By the way, we must add that the bill enshrines the use of the specific competition, a system which is not transparent enough to make it easier to name. At the same time, it enshrines the figure of the free designation, making it possible to use it in any position determined, and, of course, without union participation in that determination. Is this the kind of transparency that PP is showing? It also points out that, despite the many demonstrations of the People's Party to promote policies for reconciling working and family life, it rejects all such amendments to the said conciliation in the permits, reductions and adaptations of the working day, both with regard to the extension of the duration of permits and with the permission to care for older persons and dependents. Similarly, the People's Party rejected all amendments to the opposition that improved permits for childbirth and lactation, something difficult to understand in a country with a very serious structural problem of birth and population aging. But it was also rejected the amendments that transferred to the law the rights to work health that should be guaranteed to pregnant or nursing women. I cannot finish without mentioning the attachment the People's Party has to the deferred payments, setting out in Article 143 different remunerations; deferred remunerations that are no more than privatization of public employees' pensions by hiring private pension and employment plans or collective insurance that include retirement coverage. Finally, one hour could be talked about all the negative aspects of this bill, continuing with the policy of cuts in public services, continuing with privatization and repression. A bill that attacks the most basic democratic rights of the bodies representing workers and public workers, refusing to guarantee collective bargaining and union participation in matters that are as own to them, such as the determination of the day-to-day, wages or the control of contracts. A bill that tries to legislate to ensure opacity in access to public employment, limiting union control and preventing access to courts by draw. So far, the draft law which seeks, through Article 153.10, to grant the Administration the power to breach agreements and agreements for economic reasons. Can public workers also breach the pact for economic reasons related to their poor wages? And, of course, this bill can't know anything about the employment of our language in the Administration, refusing all initiatives aimed at ensuring the knowledge of Greek in access to the Greek civil service, as well as its priority use in the care of citizenship. A bill that does not solve any of the current and future problems and needs of public services, conditionaling on its own stability and the very existence of public services to financial stability. A bill aimed at perpetuating the precarious employment and wage of employees and public employees, fraud in recruitment, eventuality, excess workload, deterioration of public services, appointments to finger and privatization, while not resolving the salary debt the Administration has to the public workers and workers and the recovery of lost labour rights. In addition, the bill favours the lack of transparency in selective processes and in the performance assessment process. The defence of employees and public employees, as well as of citizenship in general, by vetoing the participation of trade union organisations in merit assessment, skills and skills commissions. And also a bill that works for Spaniarding not only from the Welsh Administration, but also from Spanishization in the relations between citizens and their administration. So we're going to refuse this bill. Let's keep all the amendments. And we believe that, rather than a step forward, this bill is a reversal of the rights of citizens to access public services as well as the trade union and labor rights of workers and public workers. Nothing else, thank you very much.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u10	Thank you, Mrs. Martínez. Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block Galego. Mrs. Peace.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u11	Thank you, Mr. President. Good morning, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen who accompany us from the gallery of Parliament. From the point of view of the Galego Nationalist Block Parliamentary Group, a law called public employment should regulate not only the relations of the Administration with its public employees but also their relationship with the provision of public services, and above all ensuring the quality in the provision of such public services and also in the labour relations between the Administration and its employees. First of all, we must state that, in relation to this first issue, the draft law which is now before the final debate does not preserve, from our point of view, the appropriate professionalization – which we all theoretically aspire to – of the public function in Galicia, since, from our point of view, this – let us say – institutional character, of an institutional position that must have public employees as an essential factor for the functioning of administrations, for the guarantee of impartiality in the functioning of administrations, for the guarantee of their independence of political power, is clearly questioned in various aspects of this bill that we have already denounced in the whole debate, against which the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Group of Wales formulated – and the other groups too – numerous amendments, but which have not been taken into consideration in the presentation process. And in that sense, let me make explicit mention of a number of issues which result in this bill designating a public service project, a conception of the public service more seeking an administration and public employees subject to political power that are independent in the performance and delivery of their functions, as well as in the search for greater professionalization of public employees with greater independence and guarantees of the provision of public services. In that sense, we believe that there are very worrying elements in this bill. We could start by saying that the introduction of that executive staff is one of the worrying elements, since, despite the fact that it is provided for in the Basic Statute of the Public Employee, its incorporation is not mandatory. And, of course, the way the Galician board does does not follow those criteria established by the OECD for greater professionalization of the public administration. On the contrary, from our point of view it constitutes a new gateway to political control in the Administration, even with the possibility – which has already been re-designed here – that establishes Article 33 and 34 that staff who do not have the condition of public employees may end up holding these jobs. On the other hand, the draft law does not regulate substantive aspects of what it has to do with this executive staff, such as the form of provision of these posts – which, from our point of view, should be regulated in this bill and not left to the dawn of the regulatory decision that each government wants to adopt on this matter – as well as the provision of posts, wages and other considerations on this matter. Another element of control of public employees introduced through this law is that of performance evaluation. Of course, the Galego Nationalist Block Parliamentary Group is not opposed to the fact that there are instruments to continuously validate and assess the quality of the benefits that are made from the Public Administration. But what we are strongly opposed to is that, in addition, it links itself with the new system of remuneration and that there are no guarantees of any kind, nor of union control, in order to retain some kind of arbitrariness or subjectivity to that assessment, and therefore the establishment of remunerations that have different public employees, since in the text there persist expressions of the type of "this evaluation, which will be carried out by the hierarchical superior, will be done according to the degree of interest, the initiative and effort of the public employee". We also believe that, despite what has been said in relation to free designations, they are not reduced, because in the articulate there are exceptions that will allow them to continue to be achieved practically in the same parameters that have been developed to this day in the current regulatory framework. There are other aspects, it is impossible to speak of the 209 amendments that this parliamentary group has tabled. So, if I may, I'm just going to make an impact on those aspects which, the trial of the Galician Nationalist Block Parliamentary Group, make it impossible for our group to give our support to this legal reform. There is another block of amendments and questions relating to the restitution of the rights of public employees, which, of course, are not guaranteed in this law and which, in addition, have a clear correlation with the provision of public services. All of us in this Parliament remember as one of the first initiatives of the People's Party in relation to public employees was the adoption of Law 1/2012, through which they reduced in a substantial way not only the consolidated and legally recognized rights to public employees, but even in which the right to collective bargaining was challenged, since the application of certain provisions of the Single Collective Staff Convention of the Galician Union was suspended. Well, that law itself, in a provision, established that, after two years and depending on the economic situation, the provisions of that law would be reviewed. More than two years have passed – three, indeed – we have to endure every week, practically every day in this Parliament the President of the Galician Union and other members of the Government saying that we are in economic recovery, that the Greek economy improves more than none, and yet it does not even take advantage of the adoption of this law to repeal Law 1/2012 and all the provisions it contains. It is more, I think, in a cosmetic exercise by the People's Party, that there has been some amendment to the text sent to the Government in which it was intended in some way to question this situation by partially amending questions related to the remuneration rights of public employees – of which I will speak next – but that, from our point of view, it is nothing but a bona fide propaganda. Limitations on the replacement of personnel in the Public Service are not removed, not even in areas where the impact on public services has been brutal, such as all that has to do with the provision of social services, care for older persons and dependents and minors protected by the Administration. The replacement of public employees in the field of education is not opposed, where more than 3,000 professionals have been lost, nor in the field of public health, where we see the permanent reduction of staff, with the consequent deterioration of public services. The rights of public employees, for example, are not rescinded in such important matters as sick leave situations. That is one of the issues in which the People's Parliamentary Group tabled an amendment, but which, of course, simply comes to put – as I said earlier – a small patch, but which in no way repudiates the right which, prior to the entry into force of Law 1/2012, the public employees perceived during the situation of sick leave. As far as public employees' salaries are concerned, not only is there an entirely unfair and arbitrary pay system – as I have said before – which will make public employees pay the letter according to whether they behave properly, badly or regularly, but it is also established – let's say – as a driving thread of public employees' salaries the establishment of deferred pay. Of course, we scandalise ourselves – and with great reason – when the conservatory's employer intends to pay to the canteens' employees in pretty cans, but we don't scandalise ourselves when they intend to pay some of the salaries to public employees in the form of insurance that they hire with those banks that we then have to rescue with the public money that we save in turn from reducing their salaries and entitlements to public employees. Of course, all very fair and all very fair. It must also be said, in relation to the recovery of money that they have been stolen – if they have been stolen from public employees – as a result of the decision of the Government of the State to eliminate extraordinary payments, even retroactively, which is illegal, as all judicial judgments are saying, both in the field of litigation and in the field of labour jurisdiction, which are being ruled by the State as a whole, we believe that, of course, neither the bill that has been referred to this Parliament nor the amendment formulated and accepted by the People's Parliamentary Group, if the right of public employees has been restored. Of course, I can trust you that the restitution of part of that remuneration right, which even – as I have said on many occasions – is already recognized by judicial judgments, if you trust the year 2018, in addition to clearly violating a basic collective bargaining right as is the right to negotiate wages. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u19	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Good morning, gentlemen. The draft Public Employment Act of Galicia is now being approved by this Parliament. It is a law that is called to replace both Law 4/1988, of Galician civil service, and Legislative Decree 1/2008, of the recast text of the law mentioned above, after successive amendments and which will continue to apply until the entry into force of the new law. The text now being submitted to plenary approval aims to adapt and develop the Greek legislation on this subject, within the framework set out in Law 7/2007, of the Basic Statute of the Public Employee. Ladies and gentlemen, we must remember, as does the preamble to this bill that is being brought here today, that "the regime of state civil service" – literally – "stop being a model of reference and each of the lawmakers competent in this field" – i.e. all autonomy, except for Ceuta and Melilla – "is called to design their model of public employment organization and management." That's what the preamble to this project says, then we'll actually see what it brings. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, we are talking about an extraordinarily complex and extraordinarily important subject, surely the most complex and perhaps more important law than will be dealt with in this legislation. For two reasons. First, because it affects, it determines the conditions of access and operation of the whole of the public employees of Galicia, except those who depend on the central government of the state – we are talking about approximately 121,000: 86,000 in Galicia's own board, 27,000 in municipalities and deputies, 7,600 in universities – but even more important than the previous one, which would already be of great transcendence – because it is the law that will determine how the public services of our autonomous community will be provided, except those of exclusive state competence. So we're talking about health, education, forest fires, we're talking about multiple subjects. The model established in this bill in some way will ensure for the next few years in Galicia an efficient, useful, appropriate administration to the needs and reality of Galicia or, on the contrary, to a dependent administration, unable to respond effectively to these needs. Logically, a theme as complex as this one also requires a complex law, and, in this sense, this law, with 211 articles, fifteen transitional provisions, four final provisions, is a complex law, and before it the set of parliamentary groups – 842 amendments were presented. I think it's also the most amended law in this legislation. Of the 842 amendments, it must be remembered that 37 were presented by the People's Parliamentary Group and 805 by the opposition, it must be said that with very different luck. I will begin with Article 3, which defines the principles that will inform public administrations of their actions. And I will quote three: (a) service to citizenship and general interests; (e) objectivity, professionalism and impartiality in the service; and (h) transparency. These are three principles that derive and are literally transcribed from the Basic Statute of the Public Employee at the state level. Unfortunately, ladies and gentlemen, we believe that today's bill of approval not only does it not meet these objectives, but it does not even intend to be met, on the contrary. Of course, nowhere is it stated that such a record should be, or should be, or may be, public access. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u49	For the presentation of this bill Mr. Puy Fraga has the floor.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u50	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Ladies and gentlemen, a simple look at any sociological study on the views of Galician citizens and the rest of the Spaniards on which their main political concerns are at present, shows that the main problems that their representatives have to respond to are, by this order: the unemployment and economic situation, the social crisis that this economic crisis has caused, and also the crisis of confidence in institutions, especially driven by the behavior of some members of the so-called political class that has affected most, the vast majority, of the parties that have been responsible for government in recent years. That's what the Sociological Research Center shows through its barometer. In March 2011 the concern about corruption and fraud was shared by 4.5% of Spaniards; in March next year, by 12%; in March 2013, by 44%; it continues to grow to peak in November 2014 with almost 64% of concern, and is in the last known barometer in 50%. We are therefore – and I think we can match all the groups in this – in the face of a problem that needs to be addressed. There are, of course, different ways to address this problem. The first is to ignore it, the policy of the ostrich, to hide his head and make an obvious problem. The second is to use it sectarianly and favourably. This can be done with two objectives. The first objective is to dissect the political opponent, magnifying the ball or straw in the alien eye, without seeing the beam or the whole building in his own eye. Or even, secondly, since the supposed virginity of those who have never enjoyed the confidence of citizenship to question the whole of the democratic institutions that we gave ourselves, consensually and mostly, all the Spaniards in 1978, which allowed Spain to live an unprecedented process of modernization, entry into the European institutions and access and development of our self-government. And there's a third way to address the problem of corruption, which is to do it rigorously, seriously, recognizing its complexity and seeking consensus, bringing together technical knowledge. This third way of addressing the problem is what we want and we defend as our own to eradicate corruption. Moreover, by fighting against it, we contribute to overcoming the economic crisis, because we know that institutions that do not have corrupt behaviour favor economic growth; and also because we know that we contribute to regain the confidence and legitimacy of our democratic institutions and, in our particular case, also of self-government institutions, and thus overcome that crisis of confidence in the behaviour of those we dedicate ourselves to public affairs. And we opt for this third option, recognizing clearly that the shameful and illegal behaviour of some cannot question the honest and exemplary work of the vast majority of public servants, or much less that of the democratic institutions whose action, day by day, is proving that corruption has no place in our society. And precisely because some of these people have acted illicitly and shamefully since our political formation is that we are especially committed to eradicating these behaviors of public life. A few cannot harm the honest work of thousands of public servants, thousands and thousands of people, men and women, who have contributed since 1977, since our political formation, as well as from other democratic formations, to the development of democracy in our country and to building a more just and better society. PP – as I said – is committed to the eradication of corruption. The central government, chaired by Mariano Rajoy, has promoted and approved numerous legislative measures to improve the quality of our democracy and regain the confidence of citizenship, such as the Transparency Act, the Political Party Financing Act, a strict regime for the exercise of the high office of State administration or the tightening of penalties for cases of corruption. In Galicia, the government chaired by Feijóo is also developing a plan of democratic impulse with 5 laws – of which this is one of them – which are the law of citizen participation, a law of financing the Galician political parties, the reform we are proposing today of the Council of Auditors, a law that regulates the Legal Counsel and an ambitious legislative text of transparency in Galicia's autonomous and local administration. I was saying a moment ago that corruption is a complex problem. It happens when someone takes advantage of political decision-making mechanisms for his own benefit and prejudices other people or the whole of society. It's a known and studied phenomenon. We know what the causes, their effects and how to minimize these corrosive effects are. The causes have to do with the ability of the public sector to intervene in the economy, which has grown by consensus on the construction of the welfare state in recent decades and which gives the State and public authorities a monopoly power not only of concessions, competition and works awards, but also of numerous subsidies and also of the ability to regulate, both tax regulations and administrative regulations, with decisions that therefore involve allocations or redistribution of resources in which the phenomenon of corruption can often occur. We know that its effects are especially pernicious in every order. From the point of view of the allocation, resources are badly allocated when they are intended to obtain favours and not to please them, to seek, to satisfy, the interests of consumers. So we also know that injustice takes place on the basis of who benefits and who harms, which is the whole of society. Finally, we know that it is bad for economic growth and development, and it deteriorates the institutions themselves by favoring the messianisms and extremist populisms of all kinds. And we also know that their remedies go through institutional reform, prevention, transparency, control before and after spending and sanctions. Well, the bill that I have today the honour to defend on behalf of my group is a proposal for debate and to seek consensus in this House, promoting an institutional action, and which is also consistent with some vote made in this House and supported more than by our group, by a large majority of this House, and also consistent with the contributions made by the experts who came to the Committee on the Prevention of Corruption created in this House. The Proposal for a Law Reform of the Board of Auditors and other media places first and foremost on prevention, which is together with transparency, according to which all experts have said, the best mechanism for preventing and minimizing the possibilities of corrupt practices in the public sector. This was reflected both in his speeches and in the presence in this House of the Director of OLAF, the Anti-Fraud Office of the European Union, or in the Committee of the President of the Court of Auditors of Portugal, or in the presentation also made by the representative in Spain of International Transparency. The bill – as I said – is a document for us to reach a consensus, is open to consensus, and also to technical improvements, because the procedure itself is allowing us to receive contributions from the People's Group with suggestions that we will incorporate ourselves through some amendment. What makes this bill is to pose several great principles: First, assigning the Board of Auditors responsibility for preventing corruption. In the Committee on the Prevention of Corruption which was then the superior prosecutor of the Supreme Court of Justice of Galicia, the Chief Prosecutor of the Supreme Court of Justice of Galicia, Carlos Varela, defended an opinion that we share, the view that, except for another criterion of greater foundation, in this body, in the field of the Board of Auditors, the prevention of corruption could be resided, resulting in the increase in its legitimacy and certainly in the strengthening of its functions. Because to assign this function to the Board of Auditors is to recognize, as we have done all the groups in this House and well recently when the last monitoring reports were presented, the enormous and positive work that the Board of Auditors is doing to improve the management of public resources in Galicia. And to prevent corruption, in the bill of law, what we are proposing is to clearly assign that competition to the Board of Auditors, the prevention of corruption, we specify what is the content of that competition, we set up the organizational mechanism with the creation of a new section, in which we will work that staff who, according to their own criterion of membership of the Board of Auditors, can contribute from each of the existing areas of work to developing this new competence for the prevention of corruption. And in addition, we introduced, and I think it's something important, because if you have said many times that there are also areas of corruption in the local administration, often because of the lack of personal means, that would be functional in these administrations, there are also functions in relation to the local administration, in terms of the repairs presented by the interveners, so that any local administration before raising a repair can consult a criterion that we want to be homogenous to all of Galicia on the issue of lifting repairs to the Board of Auditors itself. Secondly, as a second major proposal, this bill is intended to enhance the independence and autonomy of the Board of Auditors; firstly, by strengthening the role of the advisors themselves and also the system of choice which guarantees their maximum independence and competence. That's why we introduced in this bill hearings prior to the appointment of account advisors or which are subject to the same transparency standards as the rest of the public office of the Autonomous Administration. And so we try to promote an improvement in the independence of the staff, which from now on will no longer be selected by the system of free designation and will be selected by the system of competition. We also want to improve coordination with both Parliament and the Government of the Union. Under this rule, with this proposal the Council would become the Parliament's advisory body in economic and financial matters. Parliament could therefore ask for advice or reports on any economic and financial matter which it deems appropriate. And it also advances the deadline for accountability to approximate the audits of the Board of Auditors over time, the deadline for accountability of the Board's General Administration. And a coordinating commission is set up with the Self-Government for the purpose of monitoring and boosting the recommendations of the Board of Auditors itself. And thirdly, as a third large measure, we have introduced some novelties that the 1985 law could not contemplate, such as improving the right of audiences or public prosecutors, or the use of telematic communications. We propose, ultimately, a legislative reform that goes and wants to strengthen the commitment, not of the People's Group, but of the whole House, to the eradication of corruption, which aims to strengthen Galician self-government. And no one is spared that this proposal to strengthen the Board of Auditors takes place in the whole of a debate on the Cora report and in the set of actions in other communities that are suppressing these kinds of bodies at an autonomous level; that it wants to improve the control of public spending, not only by strengthening the control capacity that the Board of Auditors already does, but also by introducing measures that the Board of Auditors itself moves towards preventing, preventing and eradicating corruption. And we do it – I also want to emphasize this – as a bill. We're not talking about a government project, we're talking about a group that's clean offering the other groups, from the very least of institutional point of view, the possibility of this House establishing mechanisms that strengthen self-government institutions and contribute to preventing corruption. And we do it by trusting that all groups of this House will be aware that it requires the fight against all forms of public corruption, because that's what the galley society demands from us. Thank you very much.

The bill, in its explanatory statement, states that it is necessary to reform the Law of the Board of Auditors "in order to strengthen the powers of the external control body of the Autonomous Community, its independence and its effectiveness and capacity to cooperate with other institutions." However, in the articulated there is no measure to achieve such an end. The bill, in addition to attributing to the Board of Auditors competences in the prevention of corruption, entitles many others without compromising the technical, material and human resources necessary for their assumption. Among these functions, Parliament is advising on economic and financial matters, monitoring political formations, high office assets, issuing opinions and consultations and attending Parliament's consultations on budgeting and its execution, issuing optional reports at the request of the local government, analysing the demands and complaints of citizens. And as for the prevention of corruption, it must develop codes of conduct, check systems for the prevention of corruption, advise Parliament on the prevention and suppression of corruption, encourage mechanisms for the self-regulation of the private sector, initiate procedures for monitoring for complaints from anyone. I mean, it's given a lot of functions in the fight and prevention against corruption, but the Board of Auditors is not strengthened. We therefore ask what means are guaranteed to the Board of Auditors in order to fulfil all these powers assigned to it in this bill. On the other hand, the bill opens up the possibility of establishing collaborative protocols with private entities in order to improve the exercise of the functions of the Board of Auditors, but does not define what this collaboration will consist of. This means that Parliament intends to legalise the irregular situation in which most of the staff currently working on the Board of Auditors are located, as well as their working conditions. The persons currently assigned to the Board of Auditors are in a situation of free designation or as temporary staff in some cases with an age of 20 years. A staff other than professional career, internal promotion according to a system of bodies and scales, a staff without rights, a staff who has been applying for the negotiation of its own status for six years, as has been done with the Staff Regulations of the Galician Parliament and the regulatory agreement on the working conditions of officials serving the Galician Parliament. However, the bill insists on maintaining in this situation the staff that is already in the service of the Board of Auditors and using the specific contest for the appointment of the new staff. We don't understand why we don't call an OPE. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u79	Thank you very much. Comprehensive discussion of the Bill of Creation of the Official College of Dietists-Nutritionists of Galicia
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u80	This bill has been tabled with an amendment for the return of the Joint Parliamentary Group. Mr. Alfonso Rueda Valenzuela has the floor for the introduction of the bill.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u81	President, ladies and gentlemen, good morning. I appear today before this House to present on behalf of the Government the Bill of Creation of the Official College of Dietists-Nutritionists of Galicia. A request that was transferred on his day to the Council of Galicia by the professionals themselves who were at that time grouped in the so-called Galaga Association of Dietists-Nutritionists. Law 11/2001, of professional schools in Galicia, states in its Article 11 that in the territorial sphere of Galicia the creation of professional schools must be done by law of the Galician Parliament and that when the initiative is the Council of the Union the corresponding preliminary draft law will be made the request of the professionals concerned, and after appreciation by the Government of the Union of the public interest that concurs in the creation of that college, therefore, in the request they make. And, therefore and consequently, this government began the process of drafting the preliminary draft law once it was submitted to it - as it said - the request for creation by the Galaga Association of Dietists-Nutritionists for four years, together with a motivated statement attached to this request for reasons justifying, in his opinion, the creation of the school, in which he referred to the public interest, which undoubtedly has human nutrition and diet in the prevention of diseases and in the care of people's health. The next step was to submit public information to a school creation request. This was done in DG number 249 on December 30, 2011, and it has to be said that within one month no allegations were made that at least the pre-draft process was made. There was therefore no question of what was being discussed at that time, which was the public interest character of the creation and existence of this school. The following requests were made for preceptive reports and those which, without being preceptive, were considered from the Government of the United States which were convenient for the drafting of the draft law; among others, the official schools of doctors and pharmacists in Galicia. Public interest was also recognized in a report by the Health Council, which noted its status as a health profession entitled and, therefore, regulated by Law 44/2003 on the management of health professions, and also the contribution of the functions that these professionals have developed: the protection of a constitutional right to health and, therefore, to the life and integrity of people. It must be said, because it is so, that the processing of this preliminary draft law is true that it has suffered a delay, already said that it began in 2011, but the intention was to accompany at that time its approval with the processing that it began by the Government of the State of the State of the preliminary draft law on services and professional schools, in compliance – they had to do so – of the provision set out in the fourth transitional provision of the Law 25/2009, of transposition of European directives. This preliminary draft would include the delimitation of which professions should be of compulsory consideration. With which your approval, if it had taken place, would have allowed us to also adopt in Galicia a text already adapted to the demands of the new state legislation, and this was the reason to wait at that time. But, as you know, although it is true that there have been several versions of this preliminary draft of law, the truth and truth is that on the present date the approval has not been successful and, therefore, we consider from the Government of Galicia that it would be necessary to finalize the processing of this preliminary draft of law and, therefore, to create the College of Dietists-Nutritionists; that is, these professionals had to wait no longer and could have their school created. So this is the object of debate, since we understand that there were sufficient reasons – as I said – for Galician dietists-nutritionists to have since a professional school. It should be noted, therefore, firstly, that Galicia today has official schools in most of the health professions that are recognized in the law of 2003 to which I referred, in Law 44, concerning the management of health professions. To also say that if we do a small sweep by the rest of the autonomous communities we find that today there are already ten schools of dietists-nutritionists in autonomous communities, Navarre, Balearics, Aragon, Castile-A Mancha, Valencia, Basque Country, Andalusia, Catalonia, Murcia and Castile-Leon. So it's not a new figure that doesn't exist in many autonomous communities anymore. And it should also be pointed out that in this legislation, as you know, the General Courts approved Law 19/2014, of the creation of the General Council of Professional Colleges, as a representative corporation in the state sphere and with coordinating functions of all professional schools of dietists-nutritionists that exist in the national territory. I also want to say that it was adopted in Congress by all parliamentary groups. Therefore, it is clear that there is a clear tendency, a desire for collegiation, or to allow the collection of these professionals, understanding that it was sufficiently justified and therefore recognized the importance of proper nutrition, the importance of regular physical activity, to know and evaluate by present societies the connection between this, and to know and evaluate that good eating habits substantially improve people's quality of life and are determinants in the prevention of many pathologies. So we value positively, and that's why the creation of professional schools of these professionals of diet and nutrition, so that they can be organized, so that they can promote performances, so that they can be self-regulating and so they can disseminate and improve their professional activities and knowledge. It's not, of course, a purely corporate matter, but undoubtedly, and that's what justifies the approval of the project by the Government of the Union, will result in the general interest of citizens and citizens in their health and therefore in their well-being. So we present a simple text in its structure, which consists of an exhibition of motives, in which we recognize the general interest in the need to create this school, four articles, two transitional provisions and a last disposition. The object is – as I have already said – the creation of the Official College of Dietists-Nutritionists of Galicia, which will have scope for action in the territory of our autonomous community and which will group professionals in possession of official university titles in Nutrition and Dietetics, or titles from other countries that are approved. The law also states that the collection will be compulsory as long as it establishes a state law. It could not be otherwise, ladies and gentlemen, and this wording is adopted in accordance with the criteria of the Legal Counsel and how it can be verified in the draft law file and in accordance with the state laws adopted in the matter. As you know, in 2009 the Central Government has driven the amendment of article 3.2 of the Basic Law on Professional Schools to establish, and so it has been established literally, that it will be essential for the professions to be incorporated into the corresponding professional school when it so establishes a state law; that is, only a state law according to this law of the year 2009 can establish that the college of any profession is mandatory. Furthermore, this decision established in this drafting of the law of the year 2009 was referred to by the Constitutional Court, which in recent years has already annulled several precepts of autonomous laws that intended to enter into compulsory or not the collection of various professions. Therefore, we understand, according to a legal criterion that today is no longer present, no longer allows discussion after constitutional proceedings, which must be the future state school law to which I have to decide whether the collection of professionals who are now in charge of dietists and nutritionists should be mandatory or not, and what the law of Greece does is to establish that possibility if - I insist - the state law would finally agree. And in the meantime, this collection, as it couldn't be otherwise, will have a voluntary character. We therefore understand that the prejudice to free competition is going to be minimal, maximum, when the German law, which has adapted to autonomous legislation the European Services Directive, has already proposed measures such as the removal of collective barriers to the movement of professionals between communities, also the prohibition of setting guidelines on prices among schools, also the prohibition on the existence of registration quotas that exceed the administrative costs involved in processing these registrations. Therefore, the creation of this school does not come now, with this voluntary and compulsory nature if it was decided in the future, in no case to breach competition law. And finally, ladies and gentlemen, the first and second transitional provisions that they deal with are the constitution and powers of the provisional Management Commission, which must be set up for the start of the school, the constituent assembly and the approval of the statutes. And the project ends by establishing that the law will enter into force at the 20th day of its publication in the DOG, once, if so, it is approved by this Parliament. Therefore, ladies and gentlemen, on my part and on behalf of the German Government, I simply wish to express the wish that the text of this law will finally receive Parliament's approval with the greatest, as always, possible consensus, as it has counted with the unanimity of the autonomous parliaments that have adopted laws practically equivalent to what we bring here today, and that once it is in force it will serve to respond to needs, to the demands of some professionals who have long claimed the creation of this school, who develop a very important function for public health and therefore deserve and need to be collected. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u82	The defence of the amendment to the whole, you have the floor by the Joint Parliamentary Groupwoman Consuelo Martínez.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u83	Thank you, President. Well, we have tabled from the Joint Group a whole amendment to this bill and I will now explain the reasons for this amendment. By Organic Law 16/1995, on the transfer of competences from the Autonomous Community of Galicia, 5 were transferred to its Article 5 legislative development powers and executed within the framework of the State's basic legislation on public law corporations representing economic and professional interests. Article 11 of Law 11/2001 on professional schools in Galicia states that, within the territorial area of the Autonomous Community of Galicia, the creation of professional schools will be done by law of the Greek Parliament". So today we have the responsibility to approve or not the creation of a new professional school, which, in my view and beyond other considerations, does not respond to any public interest. Law 11/2001 of 18 December 2001 of the Galician Professional Colleges provides in Article 3 that professionals bound by the Public Administration will not need to be collected; although they will be compulsory for the exercise of private activity. It is clear, therefore, that the existence or not of professional schools, in general, does not affect anything about the quality of the provision of professional services that they represent or is such their public interest, but their collection would also be compulsory within the administration itself. Rather they represent corporate associations that have as their sole or most important purpose to defend the corporate interests of their professionals. And at this end there's nothing of public interest. Thus, in Article 7, the exercise of powers and collaboration with the Administration, includes: "The Autonomous Community of Galicia may entrust to the professional schools, through the corresponding convention, the carrying out of activities of a material, technical or service nature of its own competence, in accordance with the terms laid down in Law 30/1992, of a legal regime of public administrations and of the common administrative procedure". In this sense, the ESC, in its speech of 9 October 2000 on the preliminary draft law of the schools of professionals of the Autonomous Community of Galicia, recommended: "The ESC expresses its reservation on the need for entities other than associations or trade unions representing professional interests which are constituted as corporations of public law to defend the interests of a given profession and to those who are assigned purposes of public interest that could be exercised by the administration." I'd say, they should be exercised by the administration. Therefore, it is clear that they are entities representing professional interests and that if at any time they have a public interest, it is because the administration itself entrusts them with powers that should take over the administration itself. In Article 9 of the law it speaks of the functions, which are essentially corporate, without any public interest, such as exercising in its field the representation and defence of the profession; exercising disciplinary power over professionals who are collected in accordance with the terms laid down in this law and in its own statutes; exercising legal actions and taking the necessary measures to avoid professional intrusion and unfair competition; establishing standards of fees, which will have an orientation character – here I understand that there is a power which violates the right to collective free bargaining between trade unions and employers, establishing fees that should not correspond to any type of school, much less when we speak of a collection that will not be required, i.e., the school that counts with part of the professions will decide on behalf of all the many professions of things and, among others, because their standards – and how many other functions impact on the professional interests of the collegiate – not of the graduates, only on their colegescipates and on their purposes. So, we see that the creation of a school, in particular, of this school, is going to lead to the defense essentially of the professional interests of the people in school, not of all the professionals, and that their public interest in any case will depend on the powers that the administration itself grants it or does not grant it. As Mr. Rueda has already said, in article 11 it is said that "When the exercise of the legislative initiative is of the Council of Galicia, the corresponding draft bill of creation will be made the request of interested professionals and after the Government's appreciation of competing public interest in the creation of the school." As much as I read all the reports I didn't understand what the public interest is, I didn't understand it, I didn't catch it. He also says, "Only a new professional college can be created for those professions that require to be in possession of an official academic title, and also for those professional activities in which his or her exercise is conditional upon the possession of an official title that believes in and legally qualified for his or her exercise." According to this article we could set up schools in virtually any qualified profession, because all professions and all people who want to pursue a profession that requires a qualification if they are asked to participate in the work. But also the first recommendation made by the Competition Council in its report on the preliminary draft says: "It is recommended to the Council of Galicia that the draft law on the creation of the Professional College of Dietists-Nutritionists of Galicia should not be approved for the purpose of establishing an entry barrier to the exercise of the professional activity of dietitian-nutritionist, which is not the one provided for the achievement of the legitimate public purpose of protecting health." It's true, if not all health professions should be collected and, however, multiple functions or multiple health titrations are not collected. Based on its recommendation, the Competition Council, among others, on the following reasonings: it suggests that for users there are two professional categories: college professionals and non-collective professionals; the regulation of a profession does not imply the creation of a professional school, it does not say so anywhere; the recognition of a profession such as health does not imply the creation of a professional school, since, as I said, a multitude of health professions do not have a college, for example: health technicians, laboratory technicians, nuclear medicine, rays, pathological anatomy... and others only have it in some autonomous communities. But if we talk about the impact on public health, the importance of food on public health, we can also talk about the need to collect graduates in Physical Education, since physical activity is fundamental to people's health. Turning him around, we'd all end up in one school before or after. Rather, it seems that in the creation of professional schools, the political interest in them weighs more than the public interest in them. Nor does the ESC observe public interest and shows its concern at the proliferation of professional schools. In addition, if it was really of general public interest, to ensure a proper health benefit, its collection would be compulsory and would be compulsory throughout the state. It doesn't make any sense to recognize a profession and an activity as of general public interest and after the rule who wants it and who doesn't want it. It therefore clearly distorts your public interest. To conclude, I fully agree with the concern and reservation expressed by the ESC in its ruling of 9 October 2000 on the preliminary draft law of professional schools in relation to the need not to exist of entities other than trade unions and associations for the defence of professional interests. All professionals and professionals can, and do so, associate in professional groupings, which does not imply the creation of a school. And if an organization has the capacity and legitimacy to defend the interests of workers, whether they are entitled or not, it's the unions. We believe that it distorts and, of course, goes on the People's Party line, and that this is the public interest that the People's Party sees it, that it is to further weaken the roles of unions and strengthen the functions of professional schools and corporate interests, because, in addition, schools usually represent personal titles, with which the untitled staff, the less educated people, the less qualified workers will always be in lower conditions, and they cover their titles in professional schools. We therefore defend from here the role of trade unions in the representation of professionals, whether they are entitled or not. So, on the basis of the reasoned and consistent opposition to the recommendations of the Galician Competition Council and the ESC, I ask the Galician Parliament not to support this Proposal for a Law on the Creation of the Professional College of Dietists-Nutritionists of Galicia as it constitutes a barrier to the exercise of the professional activity of dietitian-nutritionist who is not proportionate to the achievement of the legitimate public purpose of protecting the health it says to pursue. And because it constitutes itself a barrier to union activity, often supplicating the role that they have in their duty to negotiate working conditions between employers and workers. Nothing else, thank you very much.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u84	Thank you very much. Non-amendant groups? [[Pause.]] For the Galician Nationalist Block, you have the floor to Mrs. Prado Colors.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2015-04-28-DSPG106.u85	Good morning. In fact, the creation and operation of professional schools is regulated in Galicia by a law of its own, the Italian law, the Law 11/2001, which regulates the basic principles and rules to which its organization and its competence must be adjusted by public law corporations representing professional interests. This law establishes three fundamental requirements when creating a new professional school. The first is that, although the initiative begins with the collective of professionals concerned – as is the case with us today, with the collective of dietists and nutritionists – creation must be done by law of this Parliament. And it states that it must be the legislative power of this Parliament which determines which professions are outside the general principle of freedom. Because when a professional school is created, it is limiting that general principle of freedom; in particular, in this case, the exercise of the free profession by establishing a regulatory framework in which it must develop. The second requirement is that it is a profession or professional activity conditioned by the possession of an official title, which also fulfils in this case the creation of this school, since we are talking about existing qualifications. In Galicia there have been for several years – in particular, four years – own studies of human nutrition and diet given at the Faculty of Sciences of the University of Compostela, on the campus of Lugo, from that in 2014 – last year – the first promotion of dietists and nutritionists trained in Galicia. We believe in the BNG that these two requirements are met unequivocally. The point is that it is up to this Parliament to legislate, to create or not the creation law of the College of Dietists and Nutritionists. In this case the initiative comes from the German Government, and it must be on the part of the affected collective, which in this case was the Association of Dietists-Nutritionists itself, created almost exclusively from its inception to achieve this end, the creation of the professional college. But as for the third requirement, which is that it responds to the public interest, that question we believe is in doubt, that there are more than reasonable doubts that this public interest is justified. I have explained to the honourable Member that he is proposing a whole series of amendments, and I am going to repeat them. He has been manifested both by the Galician Competition Council and the ESC, the Economic and Social Council, his opposition to the creation of this Professional College of Dietists-Nutritionists. The Galician Council of Competition, in particular, recommends to the board of directors that it not approve at that time the draft law on the creation of professional college. The board of directors is clear that you did not accept this recommendation and followed the process forward, so we're here on this procedure. And the Competition Council estimates that the creation of the College of Dietists-Nutritionists is not proportional to the objective of protecting people's health. And he also understands that the creation of this Official College of Dietists-Nutritionists here in Galicia is not justified, since it exists in other autonomous communities but not in the state domain. Nor does it believe that this school has its equivalent in other regulated health professions. The ESC also makes a negative assessment, at that time, of the preliminary draft law, and in particular it issues a report in which it reaffirms itself in what it has done for the German Law to create professional schools, as it expresses its reservations about the need for entities other than associations or unions. It considers that these entities preserve the professional interest of partners. And he also considers that no regulations are laid down in the regulations where the professions collected are required to be incorporated into the corresponding professional school. And the ESC also considers that, in defence of the free exercise of the profession, in compliance with the defence of competition, compulsory collection must be abolished. It also states that there is insufficient justification for the motivation of general interest and shows concern for the proliferation of professional schools. At the BNG we also express our concern that the creation of professional schools is being regulated according to the demand for part, when there is an application, and that it is not the result of a planning of professions and titles that should be able to be regulated through the creation of a professional school. We believe that the creation of new professional schools must respond to a thought-provoking design on which the Galician corporate administration must be, and not only be determined by the valuation of public interest that professionals do, that they have to prove it. And that's why we don't present a amendment to this whole bill, because, since there is no such regulation that establishes which professions are required to incorporate into the corresponding school to exercise the profession, because they perform functions that have to do with the purpose of public interest, they are leaving precisely the initiative of the interested groups. In this case, the Association of Dietists and Nutritionists has followed the procedure to get here, until this Parliament is debating the appropriateness of creating the school. We believe that, since you leave that door open that you can urge the creation of the school, so we will not oppose the creation of the school. But we have partial amendments, if today no consideration is given to the amendment to the whole tabled, partial amendments that we have tabled and which are in the direction that the collection is not compulsory, and that it is not subject to state legislation. We are talking about a law which falls within the competence of Galicia, which is the area of competence set out in the Statute for the Self-Government of Galicia, the area of competence referred to in the decree of transfers to Galicia, with which it must be clear that Galicia is responsible for this regulation. That is why we tabled amendments to that effect. The justification you just made here by the vice president of the board could be worth leaving it at the dawn of state legislation or it could be worth reaffirming that it's a Galician competition. At the BNG we believe it's a Galician competition and that we must always fight for it to be a Galician competition, and it must be clear. That's why the partial amendment we tabled, I insist, is the amendment which makes it clear that collegiation cannot be in any mandatory case, and the issue of titration... because we are in a process with everything that represents the Bologna Plan, in a process where titrations are very changing, and then it must be clear to what titrations this college is subject to. And there's also a question that seems fundamental to us, as is the use of the Greek language in a Greek professional school that has its scope in our country, in Galicia. That is why, I insist, we will not oppose the creation of this professional school, but we believe that, if today we do not accept the whole amendment, in the processing and debate in the presentation, we hope that we will take into account the amendments that we will then present.
